         │      http://jobpost-api.anihpj:8000/api/jobs/                      │
         │      → 401 Jwt is not in the form of Header.Payload.Signature ❌    │
         │                                                                    │
         │  # 3. NO token → passes RequestAuthN, blocked by AuthZ policy      │
         │  $ curl http://jobpost-api.anihpj:8000/api/jobs/                   │
         │      → 403 RBAC: access denied ❌                                   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.5  Exclusion Matching — NOT Conditions in AuthorizationPolicy
         - DEFINITION: exclusion matching lets a rule apply to everything
           EXCEPT the listed values — every positive match field has a
           negated "not" twin (paths → notPaths, principals →
           notPrincipals, and so on)
         - Why it matters: real policies always need carve-outs —
           "require a JWT on every path EXCEPT /healthz" cannot be
           expressed with positive matches alone
         - How it works: a request matches the rule only if it does NOT
           match any excluded value; exclusions sit inside the same
           from/to/when blocks and combine with positive fields
         - anihpj war story: the day require-jwt went live, kubelet
           liveness probes (which carry no JWT) started failing →
           jobpost-api pods restart-looped until /healthz was exempted
           with notPaths — the fix shown below

         [EXCLUSION FIELD REFERENCE — the "not" twin of each match field]
         ┌──────────────────────┬─────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  FIELD               │  RULE MATCHES WHEN ...                      │
         ├──────────────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  notPrincipals       │  caller SPIFFE identity is NOT in the list  │
         │  notNamespaces       │  caller namespace is NOT in the list        │
         │  notIpBlocks         │  source IP is NOT inside these CIDRs        │
         │  notMethods          │  HTTP method is NOT one of these            │
         │  notPaths            │  URL path is NOT one of these               │
         │  notHosts            │  Host header is NOT one of these            │
         ├──────────────────────┴─────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  Also available: notPorts, notRequestPrincipals (JWT identity),    │
         │  and notValues (inside when: conditions)                           │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [HEALTH-CHECK EXEMPTION — JWT required everywhere EXCEPT probes]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1                                  │
         │  kind: AuthorizationPolicy                                         │
         │  metadata:                                                         │
         │    name: require-jwt-except-health                                 │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                               │
         │  spec:                                                             │
         │    selector:                                                       │
         │      matchLabels: {app: jobpost-api}                               │
         │    action: ALLOW                                                   │
         │    rules:                                                          │
         │    - from:                          # rule 1: JWT holders only     │
         │      - source:                                                     │
         │          requestPrincipals: ["https://auth.anihpj.com/*"]          │
         │      to:                                                           │
         │      - operation:                                                  │
         │          notPaths: ["/healthz", "/ready"] ← every path BUT these   │
         │    - to:                            # rule 2: probes stay open     │
         │      - operation:                                                  │
         │          paths: ["/healthz", "/ready"]  ← no from: = anyone        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.6  TCP Authorization — Protecting Non-HTTP Workloads
         - DEFINITION: AuthorizationPolicy also protects plain TCP
           servers (PostgreSQL, Redis, Kafka) — but only with the
           TCP-valid fields: ports, principals, namespaces, ipBlocks
         - Why it matters: anihpj's PostgreSQL at postgres.anihpj:5432
           holds every user and job row; only jobpost-api and
           user-service have any business opening a connection to it
         - Identity still works on TCP: principals come from the mTLS
           SPIFFE certificate exchanged in the TLS handshake — so
           PeerAuthentication STRICT must be on for principal rules
         - ⚠️ HTTP-only fields (methods, paths, hosts,
           requestPrincipals) can NEVER match TCP traffic:
           → in an ALLOW rule, Istio ignores the whole rule — it can
             never match, so the traffic you meant to allow is DENIED
           → in a DENY rule, Istio drops only the HTTP-only fields and
             enforces the rest — the policy may DENY MORE than written

         [TCP AUTHORIZATIONPOLICY — lock PostgreSQL :5432 to two callers]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1                                  │
         │  kind: AuthorizationPolicy                                         │
         │  metadata:                                                         │
         │    name: postgres-allow                                            │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                               │
         │  spec:                                                             │
         │    selector:                                                       │
         │      matchLabels: {app: postgres}   ← the PostgreSQL StatefulSet   │
         │    action: ALLOW                                                   │
         │    rules:                                                          │
         │    - from:                                                         │
         │      - source:                                                     │
         │          principals:                                               │
         │          - "cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"                │
         │          - "cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/user-service"               │
         │      to:                                                           │
         │      - operation:                                                  │
         │          ports: ["5432"]      ← the ONLY operation field valid     │
         │                                  for TCP — no paths, no methods!   │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [VERIFY WITH psql — allowed pod connects, others get RESET]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  # From jobpost-api (on the allow-list) → works                    │
         │  $ kubectl exec deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj -- \                  │
         │      psql "host=postgres.anihpj port=5432 dbname=jobpost \         │
         │            user=anihpj" -c "SELECT count(*) FROM jobs;"            │
         │     count                                                          │
         │    -------                                                         │
         │      1847                           ✅ query returns                │
         │                                                                    │
         │  # From skills-service (NOT on the list) → TCP reset               │
         │  $ kubectl exec deploy/skills-service -n anihpj -- \               │
         │      psql "host=postgres.anihpj port=5432 dbname=jobpost" \        │
         │      -c "SELECT 1;"                                                │
         │  psql: error: connection to server ... failed: server closed       │
         │        the connection unexpectedly    ❌ reset, NOT an HTTP 403     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.7  Deep Dive — Policy Evaluation with Real Requests
         - DEFINITION: for every request, the target's sidecar evaluates
           policies in a FIXED order: CUSTOM → DENY → ALLOW → default
         - The two defaults you must memorize:
           → NO ALLOW policy targets the workload = allow-by-default
             (the mesh behaves as if authorization does not exist)
           → ANY ALLOW policy targets it = implicit deny — everything
             not explicitly matched by an ALLOW rule gets 403
         - DENY always beats ALLOW: a matching DENY ends evaluation
           immediately; no ALLOW further down can rescue the request
         - Policies in force below: deny-skills (DENY skills-service →
           user-service) and allow-jobpost (ALLOW jobpost-api GET
           /api/users/* → user-service)

         [EVALUATION WALKTHROUGH — three live anihpj requests]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  REQUEST 1: jobpost-api → GET /api/users/42 → user-service         │
         │    1. CUSTOM?  none → continue                                     │
         │    2. DENY?    deny-skills: source = skills-service? NO → continue │
         │    3. ALLOW?   allow-jobpost: source ✅ method ✅ path ✅             │
         │    RESULT: ✅ ALLOW → 200 OK (ALL conditions in one rule true)      │
         │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  REQUEST 2: skills-service → GET /api/users/42 → user-service      │
         │    1. CUSTOM?  none                                                │
         │    2. DENY?    deny-skills: source = skills-service? YES → ❌ DENY  │
         │    RESULT: 403 — evaluation STOPS; ALLOW is never even consulted   │
         │  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────  │
         │  REQUEST 3: notification-service → GET /api/users/9 → user-service │
         │    1. CUSTOM?  none        2. DENY? no match → continue            │
         │    3. ALLOW?   allow-jobpost exists but source ≠ jobpost-api       │
         │    RESULT: ❌ 403 IMPLICIT deny — an ALLOW policy targets           │
         │            user-service, so anything unmatched is rejected         │
         │            (with ZERO ALLOW policies this would have been 200)     │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    13.8  Deep Dive — Dry-Run Authorization: Test Policies Without Breaking Traffic
         - DEFINITION: annotating a policy with istio.io/dry-run: "true"
           makes Envoy SHADOW-evaluate it — recording what WOULD be
           allowed or denied while enforcing nothing
         - Why it matters: the first ALLOW policy flips a workload to
           implicit-deny; one missing rule 403s real users. Dry-run
           rehearses the change against live traffic (anihpj's
           847 req/s) with zero user impact
         - Workflow: apply annotated policy → watch shadow results in
           logs/metrics/traces → fix rule gaps → remove the annotation
           (or set "false") → policy is now enforced

         [DRY-RUN AUTHORIZATIONPOLICY — annotated YAML]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: security.istio.io/v1                                  │
         │  kind: AuthorizationPolicy                                         │
         │  metadata:                                                         │
         │    name: deny-admin-paths                                          │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                               │
         │    annotations:                                                    │
         │      "istio.io/dry-run": "true"  ← shadow mode: log, don't block   │
         │  spec:                                                             │
         │    selector:                                                       │
         │      matchLabels: {app: jobpost-api}                               │
         │    action: DENY                                                    │
         │    rules:                                                          │
         │    - to:                                                           │
         │      - operation:                                                  │
         │          paths: ["/admin/*"]   ← would block /admin — only logged  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [WHERE DRY-RUN RESULTS APPEAR — three output channels]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  1. PROXY LOGS (raise RBAC logging first):                         │
         │     $ istioctl proxy-config log deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj \     │
         │         --level rbac:debug                                         │
         │     → "shadow denied, matched policy                               │
         │        ns[anihpj]-policy[deny-admin-paths]-rule[0]"                │
         │                                                                    │
         │  2. METRICS (Prometheus scrapes merged stats from istio-agent      │
         │     :15020; graph in Prometheus or Kiali BEFORE enforcing):        │
         │     envoy_http_inbound_0_0_0_0_8000_rbac{                          │
         │       authz_dry_run_action="deny",                                 │
         │       authz_dry_run_result="denied"}  ← count of would-be blocks   │
         │                                                                    │
         │  3. TRACE TAGS (Zipkin/Jaeger span attributes):                    │
         │     istio.authorization.dry_run.deny_policy.name:                  │
         │       ns[anihpj]-policy[deny-admin-paths]-rule[0]                  │
         │     istio.authorization.dry_run.deny_policy.result: denied         │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Limitations (exam-relevant):
           • only ALLOW and DENY actions are shadowed — CUSTOM is not
           • ALLOW and DENY dry-run results are reported separately —
             check BOTH before promoting a deny-all + allow-list pair
           • shadow log lines appear only at rbac:debug proxy log level
           • experimental annotation — re-verify after Istio upgrades
           • enforcement begins ONLY when the annotation is removed or
             set to "false" — a forgotten annotation = toothless policy

    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
     CHAPTERS 12-13: DAILY WORK TAKEAWAYS (Security)
    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
    After these chapters, in your daily work you will:
      • Enable STRICT mTLS: every service call encrypted, zero code changes
      • Know that SPIFFE certs auto-rotate every 24h — no manual renewal
      • Build a deny-all AuthorizationPolicy → then allow specific paths
      • Create a zero-trust policy matrix: which service can call which
      • Validate JWT tokens at the mesh level — no auth code in services
      • Migrate safely: PERMISSIVE mode first → verify → then STRICT
      • Check certs: openssl s_client to verify mTLS between pods
      • Remember: mTLS = identity (WHO), AuthzPolicy = permissions (WHAT)
      • Know that without AuthzPolicy, ANY service can call ANY service
        even with mTLS enabled — mTLS alone is NOT access control!
      • Exempt /healthz probes with notPaths — never disable JWT auth
      • Lock PostgreSQL :5432 with a ports-based ALLOW (verify via psql)
      • Rehearse risky policies with istio.io/dry-run: "true" — read
        shadow results in proxy logs, Prometheus metrics and Kiali

    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
     CHAPTERS 12-13: ICA EXAM TIPS & QUESTIONS (Securing Workloads — 25%)
    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
    🎯 ICA EXAM FOCUS — This is 25% of the exam (official domain name:
       "Securing Workloads" — Authorization, Authentication, and Edge TLS):
      • "Encrypt all service traffic" = PeerAuthentication mode: STRICT
      • "Restrict which service can call which" = AuthorizationPolicy
      • "Validate JWT tokens at mesh level" = RequestAuthentication
      • "Protect a database/TCP port" = AuthorizationPolicy with
        to.operation.ports — HTTP-only fields NEVER match TCP traffic
      • "Test a policy safely first" = istio.io/dry-run: "true"
      • Evaluation order = CUSTOM → DENY → ALLOW; the FIRST ALLOW
        policy on a workload flips its default to implicit deny

    Q1: How do you enforce STRICT mTLS for the anihpj namespace?
    A1: Create a PeerAuthentication resource with spec.mtls.mode: STRICT in the anihpj namespace.

    Q2: What AuthorizationPolicy would allow ONLY jobpost-api to GET /api/users/* on user-service?
    A2: selector: {app: user-service}, action: ALLOW, rules: [{from: [{source:
        {principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/jobpost-api"]}}], to:
        [{operation: {methods: ["GET"], paths: ["/api/users/*"]}}]}]

    Q3: Why is mTLS alone not sufficient for security?
    A3: mTLS provides identity (WHO is calling) but not authorization (WHAT are
        they allowed to do). Without AuthorizationPolicy, ANY authenticated
        service can call ANY other service. You need BOTH.

    Q4: anihpj still has two legacy Deployments without sidecars. How do you
        reach STRICT mTLS without an outage?
    A4: Stage it: apply PeerAuthentication mode: PERMISSIVE to anihpj first
        (accepts BOTH plaintext and mTLS), confirm every edge shows locks in
        Kiali and istioctl x describe pod reports mTLS, inject sidecars into
        the two stragglers, then edit the same resource to mode: STRICT.

    Q5: Task: only istio-ingressgateway may call jobpost-api; everything else
        in anihpj is blocked. Which two AuthorizationPolicies do you apply?
    A5: (1) deny-all: namespace anihpj, spec: {} — empty spec denies all.
        (2) ALLOW: selector {app: jobpost-api}, action: ALLOW, from.source.
        principals: ["cluster.local/ns/istio-system/sa/istio-ingressgateway"].
        The deny-all flips the default; the ALLOW re-opens exactly one path.

    Q6: Only JWTs carrying role=admin may POST /api/jobs/. What do you build?
    A6: A RequestAuthentication (issuer https://auth.anihpj.com, jwksUri
        https://auth.anihpj.com/.well-known/jwks.json) plus an ALLOW
        AuthorizationPolicy: from.source.requestPrincipals
        ["https://auth.anihpj.com/*"], to.operation {methods: ["POST"],
        paths: ["/api/jobs/*"]}, when request.auth.claims[role] = admin.
        RA validates the signature; the claim check lives in the AuthZ policy.

    Q7: After turning on STRICT mTLS, every call to user-service returns 503
        (upstream connect error). A DestinationRule for user-service exists.
        Diagnose.
    A7: The DestinationRule likely sets trafficPolicy.tls.mode: DISABLE, so
        client sidecars send plaintext that the STRICT server resets. Confirm
        with istioctl x describe pod <user-service-pod> (it flags the PA/DR
        conflict), then set tls.mode: ISTIO_MUTUAL or delete the stale rule.

    Q8: Restrict PostgreSQL at postgres.anihpj:5432 so only jobpost-api and
        user-service can connect. Write and verify the policy.
    A8: ALLOW policy: selector {app: postgres}, from.source.principals
        [".../sa/jobpost-api", ".../sa/user-service"], to.operation.ports
        ["5432"]. Use ONLY ports — methods/paths never match TCP and would
        make the ALLOW rule unmatchable (traffic denied). Verify with psql:
        allowed pods run SELECT 1; blocked pods get a connection reset.

    Q9: You must land a deny policy on jobpost-api mid-day with zero risk.
        How do you test it live, and where do you read the results?
    A9: Annotate it "istio.io/dry-run": "true" — traffic keeps flowing while
        Envoy shadow-evaluates. Read results in: proxy logs ("shadow denied",
        after istioctl proxy-config log ... --level rbac:debug), the rbac
        metric with authz_dry_run_result="denied" in Prometheus/Kiali, and
        dry-run span tags in tracing. Remove the annotation to enforce.

    Q10: Kubelet probes to /healthz return 403 after you required JWTs on
         jobpost-api. Fix it without weakening auth on the real endpoints.
    A10: Use exclusion matching in the require-jwt ALLOW policy: on the JWT
         rule set to.operation.notPaths ["/healthz", "/ready"], and add a
         second rule with paths ["/healthz", "/ready"] and no from: block.
         Probes (no JWT) now pass; every other path still demands a token.

    Q11: anihpj's namespace-wide PeerAuthentication is STRICT, yet a debug
         pod's plain curl to notification-service returns 200 OK instead
         of a connection reset. kubectl get peerauthentication -n anihpj
         lists a second object, notification-legacy (selector app:
         notification-service, mode: PERMISSIVE), left over from a
         debugging session. Explain why plaintext still works, and fix
         it without touching the namespace-wide default.
    A11: PeerAuthentication precedence is narrowest-scope-wins, not
         creation order or "namespace beats workload": the workload-
         scoped notification-legacy (selector matches notification-
         service, mode PERMISSIVE) beats the namespace-wide default
         (STRICT) for that one workload only, so notification-service
         still accepts plaintext. Fix: kubectl delete peerauthentication
         notification-legacy -n anihpj — the pod then inherits the
         namespace default (STRICT). Confirm with istioctl x describe
         pod <notification-service-pod> -n anihpj (should now report
         STRICT), then re-curl plaintext and expect "connection reset by
         peer", not 200.

    Q12: istiod was accidentally scaled to 0 for about 30 hours during a
         botched Helm upgrade; the data plane kept routing on cached
         config the whole time. Now that istiod is back, calls between
         jobpost-api and user-service fail with a hard TLS handshake
         error — not a 503, not a 403. How do you confirm this is a
         cert-expiry problem, and fix it?
    A12: SVID certs are valid only 24h, and with istiod unreachable for
         over a day neither pod could renew before expiry — a plain
         PA/DR mismatch produces a 503, not a handshake failure, so
         suspect expired crypto material instead. Confirm with istioctl
         proxy-config secret deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj (the default
         cert's "Not After" timestamp is in the past) or openssl
         s_client -connect user-service.anihpj:8080 ("certificate has
         expired"). Fix: verify istiod is healthy (kubectl get pods -n
         istio-system), then force fresh CSRs with kubectl rollout
         restart deployment jobpost-api user-service -n anihpj — expired
         certs do not renew themselves without a restart.

    Q13: Security mandates that every anihpj mesh certificate chain to
         the company's existing enterprise root CA instead of istiod's
         self-signed root — but wants the least invasive change: istiod
         should keep issuing and auto-rotating the 24h workload certs,
         just under the new root. Which upgrade path do you pick, and
         what are the exact steps?
    A13: Plug-in CA certs — istiod keeps signing and rotating SVIDs,
         only the root changes (SPIRE and cert-manager/istio-csr both
         take issuance away from istiod entirely, more invasive than
         asked). Steps: (1) generate an intermediate CA under the
         enterprise root, producing ca-cert.pem, ca-key.pem, root-
         cert.pem, cert-chain.pem; (2) kubectl create secret generic
         cacerts -n istio-system --from-file=ca-cert.pem --from-file=
         ca-key.pem --from-file=root-cert.pem --from-file=cert-
         chain.pem — name MUST be cacerts, namespace MUST be istio-
         system; (3) istioctl install --set profile=default -y so
         istiod picks up the new root; (4) kubectl rollout restart
         deployment -n anihpj so every workload requests a fresh CSR
         under the new chain. Verify: istioctl proxy-config secret
         deploy/jobpost-api -n anihpj should show the enterprise
         intermediate as ROOTCA, not istiod's old self-signed default.

    Q14: The test-team ServiceAccount (cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/test-
         team) was compromised in a credential-stuffing incident and
         must be blocked from calling search-service immediately — but
         every other caller in anihpj (jobpost-api, user-service,
         skills-service, and any service added later) must keep working
         without you hand-listing every legitimate principal. Write the
         policy.
    A14: Use notPrincipals so the rule matches everyone except the
         compromised identity, instead of enumerating every legitimate
         caller: selector {app: search-service}, action: ALLOW, rules:
         [{from: [{source: {notPrincipals:
         ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/test-team"]}}]}]. Any principal
         other than test-team satisfies the rule and is allowed; test-
         team fails the match and — because this ALLOW policy now
         targets search-service — falls through to implicit deny (403).
         Belt-and-suspenders option: add a companion action: DENY rule
         with from.source.principals:
         ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/test-team"] so the block survives
         even if the ALLOW policy is later removed.

    Q15: anihpj adds a Kafka broker at kafka.anihpj:9092; only
         notification-service and search-service should publish to it.
         A teammate tries to scope a DENY policy narrowly — blocking
         skills-service only when it hits an "/admin" path — with
         rules: [{from: [{source: {principals:
         ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/skills-service"]}}], to:
         [{operation: {ports: ["9092"], paths: ["/admin"]}}]}]. After
         applying it, ALL of skills-service's Kafka traffic is blocked,
         not just calls to "/admin". Explain the bug and give the
         corrected policy.
    A15: Kafka on 9092 is raw TCP, and paths is an HTTP-only match
         field — Istio silently drops it from a DENY rule instead of
         rejecting the policy, leaving only ports: ["9092"] plus the
         principal to enforce. The rule actually reads "DENY skills-
         service on port 9092, full stop," denying MORE than intended —
         exactly the TCP DENY trap. TCP has no concept of a path, so
         there is no way to scope a TCP DENY by URL. Correct policy:
         drop the paths field and own the fact this is an all-or-
         nothing TCP block — action: DENY, rules: [{from: [{source:
         {principals: ["cluster.local/ns/anihpj/sa/skills-service"]}}],
         to: [{operation: {ports: ["9092"]}}]}]. If skills-service
         genuinely needs partial access, split it onto a separate port
         or topic-level ACL — AuthorizationPolicy cannot inspect L7
         semantics inside a raw TCP stream.

    Q16: anihpj wires an external fraud-detection CUSTOM
         AuthorizationPolicy in front of jobpost-api's POST
         /api/jobs/, calling out to fraud-check.anihpj:9000 on every
         request. A separate DENY policy permanently blocks
         skills-service from reaching jobpost-api. During a load test,
         fraud-check.anihpj's logs show it being invoked for requests
         FROM skills-service — traffic that should never reach business
         logic. Is this a bug, and does moving the DENY policy ahead of
         the CUSTOM one fix it?
    A16: Not a bug — it is the fixed Istio evaluation order: CUSTOM
         always runs first, before DENY and before ALLOW, for every
         request that reaches the sidecar, and this order cannot be
         changed by editing YAML or by policy creation order — the
         action type dictates the phase, not application sequence. So
         the fraud-check CUSTOM provider fires even for skills-service
         traffic the DENY policy will reject moments later; reordering
         the DENY policy ahead of it is not possible, and would not
         help if it were. To cut wasted external calls, filter cheaper
         callers out before CUSTOM ever runs — for example, scope the
         CUSTOM policy's own selector/from.source.notPrincipals to
         exclude skills-service directly, so a caller that is denied
         anyway never triggers the external fraud-check round trip.

━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━
PART 8: ADVANCED TRAFFIC PATTERNS                                        🔴
━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━━

Chapter 14: Canary Deployments — Safe Feature Releases
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    14.1 The Full Canary Deployment Workflow
         - Phase 1: Deploy jobpost-api:v2 with replicas: 0 (shadow deployment)
         - Phase 2: Route 5% traffic to v2, watch error rate in Kiali
         - Phase 3: Ramp up gradually: 10% → 25% → 50% → 90% → 100%
         - Phase 4: Delete v1 deployment when confident
         - Full rollback at any phase: change weight back to 0% for v2
         - DEFINITION: A canary deployment releases a new version to a
           small slice of real users first — named after the canary birds
           miners carried underground as an early-warning system
         - With Istio the % lives in a VirtualService weight field, so
           "more traffic to v2" is a 2-second YAML edit — no re-deploy,
           no DNS change, no load-balancer surgery
         - Prerequisite: a DestinationRule defining subsets v1 and v2 that
           match the pods' "version" labels — no subsets, no split
         - Golden progression for anihpj: Mirror → 5% → 25% → 50% → 100%,
           with a metrics gate between every step (see 14.5)

    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
     VISUAL: CANARY PROGRESSION — THE FULL ROLLOUT DASHBOARD
    ═══════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════════
         ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  CANARY LIFECYCLE FOR jobpost-api:v2                                │
         │                                                                     │
         │  DAY 1 ────── DAY 2 ────── DAY 3 ────── DAY 4 ────── DAY 5        │
         │                                                                     │
         │  ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐          │
         │  │  V1:100% │  V1:95%  │  V1:75%  │  V1:50%  │  V1:0%   │          │
         │  │  V2:  0% │  V2: 5%  │  V2:25%  │  V2:50%  │  V2:100% │          │
         │  └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘          │
         │                                                                     │
         │  Traffic  ████████████  ███████████░  █████████░░░  ██████░░░░░░    │
         │  to V2:      0%            5%           25%           50%      100% │
         │                                                                     │
         │  Monitor  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐  ┌─────────┐      │
         │  Check:   │ Deploy  │  │ Error   │  │ p99     │  │ No      │      │
         │           │ v2 only │  │ rate    │  │ latency │  │ errors  │      │
         │           │ (no     │  │ <1%? ✅ │  │ <300ms? │  │ for 24h │      │
         │           │ traffic)│  │         │  │ ✅      │  │ ✅      │      │
         │           └─────────┘  └─────────┘  └─────────┘  └─────────┘      │
         │                                                                     │
         │  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐   │
         │  │  🚨 ROLLBACK AVAILABLE AT ANY STAGE                         │   │
         │  │                                                             │   │
         │  │  If v2 error rate > 1% at ANY point:                        │   │
         │  │    kubectl patch vs jobpost-api-vs -n anihpj \              │   │
         │  │      --type='json' -p='[{"op":"replace",                   │   │
         │  │      "path":"/spec/http/0/route/1/weight","value":0}]'     │   │
         │  │                                                             │   │
         │  │  ⏱️  Rollback time: ~2 seconds (one YAML change!)           │   │
         │  │  👤 User impact: ZERO — v1 handles all traffic instantly    │   │
         │  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘   │
         │                                                                     │
         │  [WHAT YOU SEE IN KIALI DURING CANARY]                             │
         │                                                                     │
         │    jobpost-api (v1) ◄══ 90% traffic ═══                             │
         │         │                                                            │
         │         ├──▶ user-service    412 req/s  0.1% error   🟢            │
         │         ├──▶ skills-service   89 req/s  0.3% error   🟢            │
         │         └──▶ auth-service    312 req/s  0.0% error   🟢            │
         │                                                                     │
         │    jobpost-api (v2) ◄══ 10% traffic ═══                             │
         │         │                                                            │
         │         ├──▶ user-service     46 req/s  0.1% error   🟢            │
         │         ├──▶ skills-service   10 req/s  0.4% error   🟢            │
         │         └──▶ auth-service     35 req/s  0.0% error   🟢            │
         │                                                                     │
         │    Compare v1 vs v2 metrics side-by-side in Grafana.               │
         │    When v2 matches or beats v1 → increase % → eventually 100%      │
         └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
         [MULTI-PHASE CANARY TIMELINE with Istio YAML at each phase]

         [MULTI-PHASE CANARY TIMELINE — the exact VS YAML at each stage]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  STAGE 0 — MIRROR: v1 serves 100%, v2 gets a silent copy       │
         │    http:                                                       │
         │    - route:                                                    │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v1}            │
         │        weight: 100                                             │
         │      mirror: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2}                   │
         │      mirrorPercentage: {value: 100.0}                          │
         │    GATE → v2 digests the copied load cleanly? start canary     │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  STAGE 1 — CANARY 5%: first real users reach v2                │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v1}            │
         │        weight: 95                                              │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2}            │
         │        weight: 5                                               │
         │    GATE → v2 error rate < 1%? ramp up                          │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  STAGE 2 — CANARY 25%                                          │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v1}            │
         │        weight: 75                                              │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2}            │
         │        weight: 25                                              │
         │    GATE → errors < 1% AND p99 < 300ms? ramp up                 │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  STAGE 3 — CANARY 50%: v2 proves itself at half load           │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v1}            │
         │        weight: 50                                              │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2}            │
         │        weight: 50                                              │
         │    GATE → 24h clean at 50%? full cut-over                      │
         ├────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┤
         │  STAGE 4 — FULL 100%: v2 IS production                         │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v1}            │
         │        weight: 0                                               │
         │      - destination: {host: jobpost-api, subset: v2}            │
         │        weight: 100                                             │
         │    Keep v1 pods warm for 24h (instant rollback), then:         │
         │    kubectl delete deployment jobpost-api-v1 -n anihpj          │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CANARY DEPLOYMENT TIMELINE — full progression for anihpj]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  DAY 1: Deploy v2, zero traffic                                    │
         │  ─────────────────────────────                                     │
         │  kubectl apply -f jobpost-api-v2.yaml  (replicas: 2)               │
         │  VirtualService: v1=100%, v2=0%                                    │
         │  v2 running but receiving no user traffic                          │
         │                                                                    │
         │  DAY 2: Start canary (5% traffic to v2)                           │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────                            │
         │  Edit VirtualService: v1=95%, v2=5%                               │
         │  Monitor in Kiali: error rate for v2?                              │
         │  ✅ v2 error rate < 1% → proceed                                   │
         │                                                                    │
         │  DAY 3: Increase to 25%                                           │
         │  ─────────────────────                                             │
         │  Edit VirtualService: v1=75%, v2=25%                              │
         │  Check p99 latency for v2 in Grafana                              │
         │  ✅ p99 < 300ms, errors < 1% → proceed                             │
         │                                                                    │
         │  DAY 4: Increase to 50%                                           │
         │  ─────────────────────                                             │
         │  Edit VirtualService: v1=50%, v2=50%                              │
         │  Alert: v2 errors spike to 5% ⚠️                                  │
         │  ❌ ROLLBACK: v1=100%, v2=0% (instant, one YAML change!)           │
         │  Fix the bug in v2, redeploy, restart canary from 5%              │
         │                                                                    │
         │  (After fix) DAY 7: Full promotion                                │
         │  ─────────────────────────────────                                 │
         │  VirtualService: v1=0%, v2=100%                                   │
         │  kubectl delete deployment jobpost-api-v1                         │
         │  ✅ v2 is now production                                            │
         │                                                                    │
         │  TOTAL USER IMPACT: Zero — all changes were VirtualService edits  │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [CANARY vs BLUE-GREEN vs ROLLING — comparison table]
         ┌─────────────────┬──────────────────┬──────────────────────────────┐
         │  Strategy       │  Traffic switch  │  Best for                    │
         ├─────────────────┼──────────────────┼──────────────────────────────┤
         │  Canary (Istio) │  Gradual %       │  Risk mitigation, monitoring │
         │  Blue-Green     │  Instant 100%    │  Fast rollback, staging parity│
         │  Rolling        │  Pod-by-pod      │  Simple apps, less control   │
         │  Shadow/Mirror  │  Parallel copy   │  Zero-risk production testing│
         └─────────────────┴──────────────────┴──────────────────────────────┘

    14.2 Shadow / Mirror Traffic Testing
         - Mirror 100% of live traffic to v2 — but discard v2 responses
         - Users never see v2 responses, but v2 processes real production traffic
         - Use to: verify new jobpost-api handles production load before going live
         - Zero risk — users unaffected, v2 gets real-world testing
         - DEFINITION: Mirroring (shadowing) = the sidecar duplicates each
           request and sends the copy fire-and-forget; the mirror's reply
           is discarded and can never delay or corrupt the user's response
         - Envoy tags the copy: the Host header arrives at v2 as
           "jobpost-api-shadow", so v2's Django logs can separate
           mirrored traffic from live traffic
         - ⚠️ Mirrored requests still execute for real: if v2 handles
           POST /api/jobs it INSERTs a real row into PostgreSQL — point
           v2 at a shadow database or mirror read-only endpoints only
         [MIRROR TRAFFIC YAML + FLOW DIAGRAM]

         [TRAFFIC MIRRORING — safe production testing for anihpj]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  spec:                                                 │
         │    http:                                               │
         │    - route:                                            │
         │      - destination:                                    │
         │          host: jobpost-api                             │
         │          subset: v1           # Real users get v1      │
         │        weight: 100                                     │
         │      mirror:                                           │
         │        host: jobpost-api                               │
         │        subset: v2             # v2 gets a silent copy  │
         │      mirrorPercentage:                                 │
         │        value: 100.0           # Copy 100% to v2        │
         │                                                        │
         │  ✅ Users: 100% served by v1 (no impact)               │
         │  ✅ v2: receives all real production requests silently  │
         │  ✅ Check v2 metrics for errors before any user sees it │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [MIRROR FLOW — one user request, two deliveries]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │   user ──▶ Envoy sidecar (in front of jobpost-api)             │
         │                │                                               │
         │       ┌────────┴─────────┐                                     │
         │       ▼ LIVE             ▼ COPY (fire-and-forget)              │
         │   v1 pod                 v2 pod                                │
         │   Host: jobpost-api      Host: jobpost-api-shadow              │
         │       │                      │                                 │
         │       ▼                      ▼                                 │
         │   response ──▶ USER      response ──▶ DISCARDED                │
         │                                                                │
         │   v2 slow or crashing? user never notices — v1 already replied │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.3 Blue-Green Deployments with Istio
         - Maintain two identical environments: blue (current) and green (new)
         - All traffic on blue; green is fully deployed but receiving 0%
         - Switch: change VirtualService to route 100% to green (instant!)
         - Rollback: change back to blue (instant!)
         - Difference from canary: no gradual ramp — instant switch
         - Istio advantage: the "switch" is a YAML change, not a DNS update
         - DEFINITION: Blue-green = two complete, identical stacks; only
           the router (here, a VirtualService) decides which one is live
         - Cost trade-off: green duplicates blue's full capacity while
           both run — a canary only adds a couple of v2 pods
         - ⚠️ Shared-state gotcha: blue and green usually share the same
           PostgreSQL — schema changes must stay compatible with BOTH
           versions until blue is finally retired
         - Keep blue warm for 24h after the flip: rollback then stays as
           instant as the cut-over was
         [BLUE-GREEN SWITCHING DIAGRAM: instant traffic cut-over]

         [BLUE-GREEN SWITCHING — instant cut-over, instant rollback]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  BEFORE THE FLIP                  AFTER THE FLIP               │
         │  ───────────────                  ──────────────               │
         │  VirtualService                   VirtualService               │
         │       │                                │                       │
         │       ├─ 100% ─▶ 🔵 v1 (blue)          ├─ 100% ─▶ 🟢 v2 (green)│
         │       └─   0% ─▶ 🟢 v2 (green:         └─   0% ─▶ 🔵 v1 (blue: │
         │                  deployed, idle)                  kept warm)   │
         │                                                                │
         │  Switch   = edit ONE line in the VS  → live in ~2 seconds      │
         │  Rollback = the same edit, reversed  → equally instant         │
         │  No DNS propagation, no pod restart, no LB re-pointing         │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [THE FLIP — the entire blue-green cut-over is one line of YAML]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1                            │
         │  kind: VirtualService                                          │
         │  metadata:                                                     │
         │    name: jobpost-api-vs                                        │
         │    namespace: anihpj                                           │
         │  spec:                                                         │
         │    hosts:                                                      │
         │    - jobpost-api                                               │
         │    http:                                                       │
         │    - route:                                                    │
         │      - destination:                                            │
         │          host: jobpost-api                                     │
         │          subset: v1        # ← BLUE serving 100% of traffic    │
         │                                                                │
         │  # THE SWITCH — change that one line, then kubectl apply:      │
         │  #     subset: v2          # ← GREEN now serves 100%           │
         │  # THE ROLLBACK — the same edit, reversed:                     │
         │  #     subset: v1          # ← BLUE back, ~2 seconds, no drama │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.4  Deployment Strategy Decision Matrix — Which to Use When
         - DEFINITION: A decision matrix maps each release strategy to
           its risk, rollback speed and cost — choosing one becomes a
           lookup, not a debate
         - Why it matters: the ICA exam (and 2 A.M. on-call reality)
           hands you a scenario — "high-risk rewrite", "DB migration",
           "tiny patch" — and expects the matching strategy, every time
         - How to read it: risk = worst-case user impact mid-rollout;
           rollback = time to undo; cost = extra infrastructure kept
           running while the rollout is in flight

         [DECISION MATRIX — four strategies scored for anihpj]
         ┌────────────┬─────────┬──────────┬─────────┬────────────────────┐
         │ Strategy   │ Risk    │ Rollback │ Cost    │ Use at anihpj for  │
         ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
         │ Canary     │ 🟡 Low  │ ~2s: v2  │ + a few │ jobpost-api v2:    │
         │ (weights)  │ 5% blast│ weight→0 │ v2 pods │ risky matching-    │
         │            │ radius  │          │         │ logic rewrite      │
         ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
         │ Blue-Green │ 🟡 Low  │ ~2s: flip│ 2× full │ user-service:      │
         │ (VS flip)  │ all-or- │ VS back  │ stack   │ PostgreSQL schema  │
         │            │ nothing │          │ (blue + │ migration needing  │
         │            │         │          │ green)  │ an instant undo    │
         ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
         │ Rolling    │ 🔴 High │ minutes: │ ~1×     │ location-service:  │
         │ (K8s only) │ no %    │ rolling  │ (surge  │ trivial stateless  │
         │            │ control │ restart  │ pods)   │ patch bump         │
         ├────────────┼─────────┼──────────┼─────────┼────────────────────┤
         │ Shadow /   │ 🟢 Zero │ n/a: rm  │ + v2    │ skills-service:    │
         │ Mirror     │ users   │ mirror   │ pods    │ prove the 8.1%-    │
         │            │ never   │ stanza   │         │ error fix under    │
         │            │ hit v2  │          │         │ real load, safely  │
         └────────────┴─────────┴──────────┴─────────┴────────────────────┘

         [DECISION FLOW — pick a strategy in four questions]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  Q1  Must users be 100% shielded while v2 is tested?           │
         │        │ YES → SHADOW / MIRROR  (then come back to Q2)         │
         │        ▼ no                                                    │
         │  Q2  All-or-nothing change (DB schema / infra) + instant undo? │
         │        │ YES → BLUE-GREEN                                      │
         │        ▼ no                                                    │
         │  Q3  Risky code you want to watch under partial real load?     │
         │        │ YES → CANARY  5% → 25% → 50% → 100%                   │
         │        ▼ no                                                    │
         │  Q4  Boring, low-risk, stateless patch?                        │
         │          YES → plain Kubernetes ROLLING UPDATE                 │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    14.5  Deep Dive — Automated Canary: Metrics-Gated Promotion and Rollback
         - DEFINITION: A metrics-gated canary promotes (or rolls back) v2
           automatically, based on Prometheus numbers instead of a human
           staring at Grafana for five days
         - Why it matters: humans miss 3 A.M. error spikes; a controller
           checking every minute does not — and it rolls back in seconds,
           before the pager even fires
         - How it works: a loop queries Prometheus → compares v2 against
           the gates (and against v1's live baseline) → edits the
           VirtualService weights up on pass, to zero on fail
         - Set the gates BEFORE the rollout starts — deciding thresholds
           mid-incident is how bad deploys reach 100%

         [PROMOTION GATES — what jobpost-api v2 must prove at each step]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  GATE 1  ERROR RATE  v2 5xx ratio < 1%                         │
         │                      AND not > 2% above v1's live rate         │
         │  GATE 2  LATENCY     v2 p99 < 300ms                            │
         │                      AND not > 2× v1's p99 (baseline 234ms)    │
         │  GATE 3  SOAK TIME   24h clean at the current weight           │
         │                      before ramping to the next step           │
         │  ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────        │
         │  ALL gates pass → promote:  5% → 25% → 50% → 100%              │
         │  ANY gate fails → rollback: v2 weight → 0  (~2 seconds)        │
         │                                                                │
         │  # Gate query — v2 error ratio over the last 5 minutes:        │
         │  sum(rate(istio_requests_total{destination_service_name=       │
         │    "jobpost-api",destination_version="v2",                     │
         │    response_code=~"5.."}[5m]))                                 │
         │  / sum(rate(istio_requests_total{destination_service_name=     │
         │      "jobpost-api",destination_version="v2"}[5m]))             │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [INSTANT ROLLBACK — the one-liner to memorize]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  kubectl patch vs jobpost-api-vs -n anihpj --type='json' \     │
         │    -p='[{"op":"replace",                                       │
         │         "path":"/spec/http/0/route/1/weight","value":0}]'      │
         │                                                                │
         │  # route/1 = the v2 entry in the VS route list                 │
         │  # With v2 at weight 0, Envoy sends ALL traffic to v1 —        │
         │  # weights are relative proportions, so v1's number does       │
         │  # not need to be edited back to 100                           │
         │  # Propagation ~2s. Pods untouched. Users never notice.        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [AUTOMATED CANARY LOOP — what a controller does every minute]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  T+0m : set v2 weight = 5, start the clock                     │
         │  T+1m : query Prometheus → all gates pass? ✅ keep watching    │
         │  ...                                                           │
         │  T+24h: 24h clean → weight 25 → re-gate → 50 → re-gate → 100   │
         │  ANY minute a gate fails:                                      │
         │    → v2 weight = 0        (automatic — no human in the loop)   │
         │    → alert #anihpj-oncall with the failing metric attached     │
         │    → rollout marked FAILED; fix v2, start again from MIRROR    │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Flagger (CNCF, Flux family): you declare a Canary custom
           resource holding these gates; Flagger then OWNS the
           VirtualService — it creates it, walks the weights up step by
           step, runs the Prometheus checks between steps, and flips the
           weight back to 0 on failure. One page of YAML replaces five
           days of dashboard-watching.
         - Argo Rollouts: swaps your Deployment for a Rollout CRD whose
           strategy.canary.trafficRouting.istio block points at your VS
           and DR; AnalysisTemplates run PromQL gates between steps and
           abort automatically on failure. Pairs naturally with Argo CD
           for GitOps-driven progressive delivery.


Chapter 15: Rate Limiting and Envoy Filters
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    15.1 Local Rate Limiting (Per-Pod)
         - Limit how many requests each jobpost-api pod accepts per second
         - Uses Envoy's built-in local rate limiter
         - Applied via EnvoyFilter resource
         - Example: max 100 req/s per pod → protects Django from overload
         [EnvoyFilter YAML for local rate limiting on jobpost-api]

         [ENVOYFILTER YAML — local rate limit 100 req/s on jobpost-api]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3    # EnvoyFilter never    │
         │ kind: EnvoyFilter                           # graduated past alpha │
         │ metadata:                                                          │
         │   name: jobpost-api-local-ratelimit                                │
         │   namespace: anihpj                 # ← lives beside the workload  │
         │ spec:                                                              │
         │   workloadSelector:                                                │
         │     labels:                                                        │
         │       app: jobpost-api              # ← ONLY these pods patched    │
         │   configPatches:                                                   │
         │   - applyTo: HTTP_FILTER            # add to the HTTP filter chain │
         │     match:                                                         │
         │       context: SIDECAR_INBOUND      # ← inbound = protect Django   │
         │       listener:                                                    │
         │         filterChain:                                               │
         │           filter:                                                  │
         │             name: envoy.filters.network.http_connection_manager    │
         │     patch:                                                         │
         │       operation: INSERT_BEFORE      # slot in before the router    │
         │       value:                                                       │
         │         name: envoy.filters.http.local_ratelimit                   │
         │         typed_config:                                              │
         │           "@type": type.googleapis.com/udpa.type.v1.TypedStruct    │
         │           # NOTE: type_url is ONE long line in the real file       │
         │           type_url: type.googleapis.com/envoy.extensions           │
         │                     .filters.http.local_ratelimit.v3               │
         │                     .LocalRateLimit                                │
         │           value:                                                   │
         │             stat_prefix: http_local_rate_limiter                   │
         │             token_bucket:                                          │
         │               max_tokens: 100       # ← burst ceiling (bucket cap) │
         │               tokens_per_fill: 100  # ← tokens added per refill    │
         │               fill_interval: 1s     # ← refill period → 100 req/s  │
         │             filter_enabled:         # % of traffic EVALUATED       │
         │               default_value:                                       │
         │                 numerator: 100                                     │
         │                 denominator: HUNDRED                               │
         │             filter_enforced:        # % actually LIMITED (429s)    │
         │               default_value:                                       │
         │                 numerator: 100                                     │
         │                 denominator: HUNDRED                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Tuning: change the numbers → kubectl apply; delete the
           EnvoyFilter → the limit disappears instantly (no restart)
         - filter_enabled vs filter_enforced enables a DRY-RUN: evaluate
           100% but enforce 0% → watch would-be 429s in metrics first

         [VERIFY — prove the limiter is live, then trip it]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ $ istioctl proxy-config listener deploy/jobpost-api \        │
         │     -n anihpj -o json | grep local_ratelimit                 │
         │ "name": "envoy.filters.http.local_ratelimit"   ← filter live │
         │                                                              │
         │ $ for i in $(seq 1 150); do \                                │
         │     curl -s -o /dev/null -w "%{http_code}\n" \               │
         │       http://jobpost-api:8000/jobs/; done | sort | uniq -c   │
         │     100 200        ← first 100 grabbed a token               │
         │      50 429        ← bucket empty → rejected by Envoy        │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [LOCAL RATE LIMITING — per-pod bucket diagram]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  LOCAL rate limit: 100 req/s per Pod                              │
         │                                                                    │
         │  jobpost-api has 3 pods:                                           │
         │                                                                    │
         │  [Pod 1]  ┌─────────────────────────┐                             │
         │           │  Token bucket: 100 tok/s │  ← 100 allowed/sec        │
         │           │  ████████░░░░░  82/100   │  ← 82 used this second    │
         │           └─────────────────────────┘                             │
         │                                                                    │
         │  [Pod 2]  ┌─────────────────────────┐                             │
         │           │  Token bucket: 100 tok/s │  ← own independent bucket │
         │           │  ██████████████  100/100 │  ← AT LIMIT → 429 returned│
         │           └─────────────────────────┘                             │
         │                                                                    │
         │  [Pod 3]  ┌─────────────────────────┐                             │
         │           │  Token bucket: 100 tok/s │                             │
         │           │  ███░░░░░░░░░░░  30/100  │  ← 30 used this second    │
         │           └─────────────────────────┘                             │
         │                                                                    │
         │  Total capacity: 3 pods × 100 = 300 req/s                         │
         │  Pod 2 hit its limit → returns 429 Too Many Requests               │
         │  (Other pods still serving — local limit, not global!)            │
         │                                                                    │
         │  EnvoyFilter YAML (simplified):                                    │
         │  spec:                                                             │
         │    configPatches:                                                  │
         │    - applyTo: HTTP_FILTER                                         │
         │      match: {context: SIDECAR_INBOUND}                            │
         │      patch:                                                        │
         │        value:                                                      │
         │          typed_config:                                             │
         │            maxTokens: 100                                          │
         │            tokensPerFill: 100                                      │
         │            fillInterval: 1s                                       │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.2 Global Rate Limiting (Shared Counter)
         - Rate limit by source IP, user, API key across ALL pods combined
         - Requires a rate limit service (e.g., Redis-backed rate limiter)
         - Example: limit each unique IP to 1000 requests/minute to /jobs/
         [ARCHITECTURE DIAGRAM: Envoy → rate limit service → Redis]

         [GLOBAL RATE LIMITING — architecture for anihpj]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │                                                                    │
         │  Request from IP 203.0.113.42 → jobpost-api                       │
         │                                                                    │
         │  [jobpost-api Pod] ──▶ Envoy sidecar                              │
         │                              │                                     │
         │                    "Check rate limit for this IP"                 │
         │                              │  gRPC check                        │
         │                              ▼                                     │
         │                  [Rate Limit Service]  ← deployed separately      │
         │                              │  "Has IP 203... exceeded limit?"   │
         │                              ▼                                     │
         │                      [Redis Cache]                                 │
         │                      IP: 203.0.113.42                              │
         │                      count: 987/1000  ← under limit ✅             │
         │                              │                                     │
         │                    Response: "ALLOWED" + remaining: 13            │
         │                              │                                     │
         │                              ▼                                     │
         │               Request forwarded to Django ✅                       │
         │                                                                    │
         │  REQUEST 1001 from same IP:                                        │
         │  Redis: count: 1001/1000 → RATE_LIMITED!                          │
         │  Envoy returns: 429 Too Many Requests  ❌                          │
         │  Retry-After: 60s header added                                     │
         │                                                                    │
         │  GLOBAL vs LOCAL comparison:                                       │
         │  Local:  each pod has its own counter (3 pods = 300 req/s total)  │
         │  Global: ALL pods share ONE counter (3 pods = 1000 req/min TOTAL) │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - When do I ACTUALLY need global limiting? Decision note:
           • the limit describes a CLIENT ("each IP: 1000 req/min",
             "free tier: 100 calls/day") → it must hold across ALL
             pods at once → GLOBAL is the only correct answer
           • the limit protects one POD's capacity ("a Django worker
             melts past 100 req/s") → LOCAL is enough — and simpler
           • HPA changes pod count → LOCAL totals drift silently
             (3 pods = 300 req/s; autoscaled to 6 pods = 600 req/s)
             → if the business number must stay fixed, go GLOBAL
           • can't run Redis + an extra deployment? → stay LOCAL

         [GLOBAL vs LOCAL — quick decision table for anihpj]
         ┌────────────────────────────────┬───────────────────────────┐
         │ Situation                      │ Pick                      │
         ├────────────────────────────────┼───────────────────────────┤
         │ Protect Django pods from melt  │ LOCAL  (per-pod bucket)   │
         │ "1000 req/min per source IP"   │ GLOBAL (shared counter)   │
         │ Free vs paid API-key quotas    │ GLOBAL (per-key counter)  │
         │ Quick guardrail, zero infra    │ LOCAL                     │
         │ Exact org-wide request budget  │ GLOBAL                    │
         └────────────────────────────────┴───────────────────────────┘

         - Redis sizing: each active limit key (one per unique IP /
           user / API key in the current window) is a tiny counter
           with a TTL — budget ~100 bytes per key; 1,000,000 live
           client IPs ≈ ~100 MB, so one small Redis (256 MB) covers
           anihpj with headroom; also budget the ~1-5ms gRPC check
           per request into your p99 (234ms today) before enabling

    15.3 Traffic Shaping — Controlling Latency
         - Add deliberate delays to slow down specific clients or paths
         - Use to enforce SLAs: "background batch jobs get max 10 req/s"
         - vs regular user traffic gets full speed

         [TRAFFIC SHAPING — priority lanes for anihpj requests]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │  PROBLEM: Background job scraper hitting /jobs/ exhausts resources │
         │                                                                    │
         │  SOLUTION: Distinguish traffic by header, apply different limits   │
         │                                                                    │
         │  Regular user request:         Batch/scraper request:             │
         │  Header: x-client: browser     Header: x-client: batch-job        │
         │          │                             │                           │
         │          ▼                             ▼                           │
         │  ┌──────────────┐           ┌──────────────────────┐              │
         │  │ No rate limit │           │ EnvoyFilter: add 100ms│             │
         │  │ full speed   │           │ delay per request    │             │
         │  │ 0ms overhead │           │ + max 10 req/s       │             │
         │  └──────────────┘           └──────────────────────┘              │
         │                                                                    │
         │  Result: batch jobs work but can't starve real users ✅            │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - How the 100ms brake is actually built: match the header in
           a VirtualService and reuse fault-injection delay as a
           shaping tool (delay applies BEFORE the request hits Django)

         [SHAPING YAML — slow lane for batch traffic (VirtualService)]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1                            │
         │ kind: VirtualService                                          │
         │ metadata:                                                     │
         │   name: jobpost-api-shaping                                   │
         │   namespace: anihpj                                           │
         │ spec:                                                         │
         │   hosts: ["jobpost-api"]                                      │
         │   http:                                                       │
         │   - match:                          # LANE 1: the slow lane   │
         │     - headers:                                                │
         │         x-client:                                             │
         │           exact: batch-job          # ← scrapers / batch jobs │
         │     fault:                                                    │
         │       delay:                                                  │
         │         percentage: {value: 100}                              │
         │         fixedDelay: 100ms           # ← the 100ms brake       │
         │     route:                                                    │
         │     - destination: {host: jobpost-api}                        │
         │   - route:                          # LANE 2: everyone else   │
         │     - destination: {host: jobpost-api}    # full speed, 0ms   │
         └───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         - Pair the slow lane with a local rate limit EnvoyFilter that
           matches the same header to enforce the "max 10 req/s" half

    15.4  Deep Dive — Token Bucket Algorithm Explained
         - DEFINITION: a token bucket is a jar of "permission slips"
           (tokens); every request must grab one token to pass — an
           empty jar means Envoy answers 429 on the spot and Django is
           never called
         - Why it matters: the three knobs (maxTokens, tokensPerFill,
           fillInterval) are the ONLY rate-limit math the exam tests —
           misread fillInterval: 60s as 1s and your "100 req/s" limit
           silently becomes 100 req/MINUTE
         - How it works:
           • fillInterval fires like a metronome → adds tokensPerFill
             tokens, capped at maxTokens (the bucket never overflows)
           • each arriving request consumes exactly 1 token → forwarded
           • bucket empty → instant 429 Too Many Requests, ~0ms cost
         - The two derived numbers to memorize:
           • sustained rate = tokensPerFill ÷ fillInterval
             → 100 ÷ 1s = 100 req/s for each jobpost-api pod
           • burst ceiling = maxTokens → a full, idle bucket can drain
             all at once, absorbing a one-off spike of that size

         [TOKEN BUCKET — millisecond walkthrough at 100 tok/s]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CONFIG: maxTokens: 100  tokensPerFill: 100  fillInterval: 1s   │
         │                                                                │
         │ T=0ms:      bucket FULL   [██████████] 100/100 tokens          │
         │ T=0-300ms:  burst — 100 requests land, each takes 1 token      │
         │ T=300ms:    bucket EMPTY  [░░░░░░░░░░]   0/100                 │
         │ T=310ms:    request #101 → no token → 429 (Envoy rejects)      │
         │ T=310-999:  every further request → 429, Django untouched      │
         │ T=1000ms:   refill tick: +100 tokens, capped at 100 → FULL     │
         │ T=1001ms:   traffic flows again ✓                              │
         │                                                                │
         │ BURST TUNING (same 100 req/s sustained, different spikes):     │
         │   maxTokens: 100, tokensPerFill: 100 → burst 100, steady 100/s │
         │   maxTokens: 300, tokensPerFill: 100 → burst 300 after a few   │
         │     quiet seconds (bucket saves up), still 100/s average       │
         │   rule: maxTokens = tokensPerFill  → uniform traffic           │
         │         maxTokens > tokensPerFill  → spiky browser traffic     │
         │                                                                │
         │ LOCAL vs GLOBAL MATH (identical YAML, different totals):       │
         │   LOCAL on 3 jobpost-api pods: 3 × 100 = 300 req/s effective   │
         │   HPA scales to 6 pods → silently becomes 600 req/s!           │
         │   GLOBAL: 100 req/s TOTAL, no matter how many pods exist       │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    15.5  Deep Dive — Extending Envoy: WasmPlugin vs Lua EnvoyFilter
         - DEFINITION: when no built-in CRD (VirtualService,
           AuthorizationPolicy, Telemetry) can express your logic, Istio
           lets you run YOUR OWN CODE inside the sidecar — through
           exactly two doors:
           • WasmPlugin CRD (extensions.istio.io/v1alpha1) — loads a
             compiled WebAssembly module into Envoy's sandboxed VM
           • Lua script via EnvoyFilter — injects a small interpreted
             function straight into the HTTP filter chain
         - Why it matters: custom auth token formats, bespoke request
           tagging, odd legacy protocols — real meshes hit these; the
           exam expects you to know WHICH mechanism fits and what each
           one risks
         - How they ship: istiod distributes both; the Wasm module is
           pulled from an OCI registry by istio-agent (like a container
           image), while the Lua source travels inline inside the
           EnvoyFilter YAML itself
         - ⚠️ Extensions run on EVERY matching request — exhaust the
           built-in CRDs first; custom code is yours to debug at 3am

         [WASMPLUGIN — annotated YAML (the REAL extension CRD)]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ apiVersion: extensions.istio.io/v1alpha1    # ← WasmPlugin's home  │
         │ kind: WasmPlugin                                                   │
         │ metadata:                                                          │
         │   name: jobpost-header-guard                                       │
         │   namespace: anihpj              # plugin scoped to anihpj ns      │
         │ spec:                                                              │
         │   selector:                                                        │
         │     matchLabels:                                                   │
         │       app: jobpost-api           # ← attach to these sidecars      │
         │   url: oci://registry.anihpj.dev/wasm/header-guard:v1.2.0          │
         │                                  # ← module pulled like an image   │
         │   sha256: 4f2a91c0...            # ← pin the binary (supply chain) │
         │   imagePullPolicy: IfNotPresent                                    │
         │   phase: AUTHN                   # ← where in the filter chain:    │
         │                                  #   AUTHN = before Istio authn    │
         │                                  #   AUTHZ = before Istio authz    │
         │                                  #   STATS = after authz (metrics) │
         │   priority: 10                   # higher runs earlier in a phase  │
         │   failStrategy: FAIL_CLOSE       # plugin crash → block traffic    │
         │                                  # (FAIL_OPEN = let traffic pass)  │
         │   pluginConfig:                  # ← handed to the module as JSON  │
         │     header: x-anihpj-guard                                         │
         │     value: "checked"                                               │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [LUA ENVOYFILTER — inline script tagging anihpj requests]
         ┌───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ apiVersion: networking.istio.io/v1alpha3                          │
         │ kind: EnvoyFilter                                                 │
         │ metadata:                                                         │
         │   name: jobpost-lua-tag                                           │
         │   namespace: anihpj                                               │
         │ spec:                                                             │
         │   workloadSelector:                                               │
